<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **invariant-all-cut-thinness**: A spanning tree can satisfy the inverse-connectivity cut bound simultaneously for every nonempty proper vertex subset, with a universal constant independent of graph size.
- **invariant-positive-weight-rank-preservation**: The auxiliary isotropic representation preserves the incidence rank of every edge subset because its common linear map is invertible and its individual edge scalings are positive.
- **invariant-same-subset-controls-both-systems**: The selected edge subset must satisfy both the auxiliary matrix lower bound that certifies tree packing and the Laplacian upper bound that controls cuts.
- **invariant-cut-test-scope**: Shortcut matrices obey upper bounds on proper nonempty cuts; these bounds do not assert domination by the graph Laplacian in the positive semidefinite order.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-use-uniform-runtime-guarantee**: Treat the extraction proof as an implementation with a uniform running-time guarantee. -> The threshold-stabilizing cover scale can depend arbitrarily strongly on the fixed graph, and the proof gives no computational-cost guarantee.
- **anti-use-weighted-edge-collapse**: Replace parallel edges by a weighted edge while retaining the same tree-packing certificate. -> Equal Laplacians can conceal different numbers of individually available edges and therefore different edge-disjoint tree packings.
- **anti-use-full-vertex-cut**: Extend the shortcut cut constraint to the entire vertex set. -> The whole-vertex cut has no crossing edges, so its proposed upper bound contradicts full positive definiteness.
- **anti-use-independent-system-selections**: Choose separate edge subsets for cut control and packing control, then assume a joint certificate. -> The proof certifies both requirements only for the same selected subset; separate selections need not preserve both properties together.
- **anti-use-unrestricted-network-model**: Apply the thin-tree conclusion directly to directed or disconnected network inputs. -> Such inputs do not satisfy the graph and connectivity hypotheses of the theorem.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main thin-tree existence proof obtains the extraction matrix through continuous self-maps and a convergent subsequence of fixed points. It does not furnish a finite constructive procedure with a computational contract suitable for this panel. The source explicitly states: “The construction makes no claim about its computational cost.” The finite-bit construction is assigned to a separate companion article. Finite checks of auxiliary packing or splitting examples would not decide the full existence theorem.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The thin-tree theorem supplies a structural existence guarantee, but the extraction argument does not establish computational cost suitable for a software construction contract. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The graph model controls static cuts and connectivity. It supplies no evolution rule, collision model, or simulation update procedure. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The shortcut matrix is constrained on indicator vectors of cuts. This does not furnish a general matrix validation utility that can replace positive-semidefinite comparison. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The edge-disjoint-path lemma gives a finite constructive subroutine: augment an integral antisymmetric flow along available paths, then extract paths from its positive-flow edges. This construction concerns the auxiliary path lemma; it does not implement the main thin-tree existence proof. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The packing argument requires each parallel edge to retain its identity. That requirement constrains graph representations, but the paper does not construct a storage structure or establish data-access guarantees. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The vector-splitting result guarantees the existence of a subset under matrix and vector-norm assumptions. It gives no accelerator architecture, precision contract, or hardware selection procedure. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The independent-vector theorem provides a positive-probability spectral event. Its conclusion supplies neither a cryptographic security property nor an adversarial failure guarantee. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The isotropic representation normalizes incidence vectors from a union of edge-disjoint spanning trees while preserving subset ranks. It does not define a learning objective, training procedure, or statistical prediction guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper explains how thin trees can support asymmetric traveling salesman rounding, but that application also requires control of tree cost. The main theorem supplies the cut guarantee without a cost-constrained rounding construction. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The simultaneous response lemma chooses a matrix for each matrix in a compact domain. It specifies no local communication model or distributed procedure for coordinating those choices. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The fixed-point theorem supports the mathematical existence argument on compact convex sets. It does not by itself provide a further engineering construction or a finite stopping criterion for finding the required matrix. |

Extract such a path and subtract one on its edges. Repeating extracts $q$ paths. <a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-main-graph-regime** (hypothesis): The thin-tree conclusion concerns finite undirected loopless multigraphs with positive integer edge connectivity and at least two vertices. Blocked use: ['Apply the theorem directly to directed networks.', 'Claim the bound for disconnected inputs or graphs outside the stated vertex regime.']
- **limit-indexed-edge-resources** (formal_scope): Parallel edges must remain separate resources when selecting subsets and certifying edge-disjoint spanning-tree packings; equality of weighted Laplacians does not preserve the packing supply. Blocked use: ['Collapse parallel edges into a weighted edge and transfer the original packing certificate.']
- **limit-cut-tests-versus-semidefinite-order** (formal_scope): An upper bound tested on cut indicators does not establish an upper bound in the positive semidefinite order. Blocked use: ['Replace a cut inequality by a semidefinite inequality without an additional proof.']
- **limit-shortcut-proper-cuts** (edge_case): The shortcut cut constraint excludes the whole vertex set because imposing it there contradicts full positive definiteness. Blocked use: ['Include the whole vertex set among shortcut cut tests.', 'Require the shortcut matrix to be dominated by the graph Laplacian as an unstated consequence.']
- **limit-hierarchy-marking-and-cutoff** (hypothesis): The polynomial shortcut estimate requires a locally connected hierarchy, the stated lower bound on each marked parent boundary relative to its full boundary, and a connectivity parameter above the absolute cutoff. Blocked use: ['Apply the marked-boundary estimate to arbitrary nested sets without local connectivity.', 'Drop the marked-node boundary ratio or assume the estimate below its cutoff.']
- **limit-extraction-large-packing** (hypothesis): Low-resistance extraction assumes a tree-packable graph on at least two vertices and a sufficiently large packing parameter. The absolute cutoff does not make arbitrary small packing parameters admissible. Blocked use: ['Invoke the extraction estimate for an arbitrary connected graph with insufficient packing.', 'Use the large-packing resistance threshold as a theorem for every positive packing parameter.']
- **limit-response-discontinuity** (nonconstructive): The simultaneous response need not depend continuously on its input. Extraction interpolates finitely many responses, obtains fixed points, and passes to a convergent subsequence. Blocked use: ['Apply the continuous fixed-point theorem directly to the unmodified response selection.', 'Treat the limiting extraction argument as an already specified finite stopping procedure.']
- **limit-no-extraction-runtime-contract** (nonconstructive): The finite-cover scale needed to stabilize the chosen threshold can be arbitrarily small for a fixed graph. The extraction construction makes no computational-cost claim. Blocked use: ['Infer a uniform algorithmic running time from the absolute connectivity cutoff.', 'Advertise a latency guarantee for exact extraction based on this proof.']
- **limit-auxiliary-positive-rank-preservation** (hypothesis): The auxiliary representation comes from a union of edge-disjoint spanning trees and preserves subset ranks through an invertible common map and strictly positive individual edge scalings. Blocked use: ['Substitute arbitrary isotropic vectors for the auxiliary representation.', 'Allow vanishing edge scalings and retain the asserted subset-rank certificate.']
- **limit-projected-trace-certificate** (hypothesis): The packing lower bound requires the specified rank-preserving auxiliary vectors and a positive semidefinite lower bound for the selected edge subset. Blocked use: ['Infer a packing count from a covariance lower bound for vectors unrelated to the graph incidence ranks.', 'Treat the rounded packing lower bound as an exact maximum packing count.']
- **limit-splitting-common-subset** (formal_scope): Cut control and packing control must hold for the same selected edge subset. The stacked covariance can have nonvanishing cross blocks, so the proof bounds the full covariance rather than assuming block independence. Blocked use: ['Choose unrelated subsets for the cut and packing requirements and assert their joint feasibility.', 'Discard covariance cross blocks without justification.']
- **limit-sparsification-threshold-and-rounding** (hypothesis): Packing-preserving sparsification applies above its absolute cutoff, with the specified error parameters and rounded retained packing count. Its cut and ratio bounds belong to that regime. Blocked use: ['Iterate the step below its cutoff.', 'Ignore the floor in the retained packing count when certifying the contraction ratio.']
- **limit-mss-distributional-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The independent-vector theorem assumes finite supports, independence, exactly isotropic total covariance, and the stated expected squared-norm bounds. Its conclusion is existence of a positive-probability successful outcome. Blocked use: ['Apply the theorem unchanged to dependent vectors or nonisotropic covariance.', 'Read positive probability as a high-probability success guarantee or a sampling runtime bound.']
- **limit-polynomial-limit-regime** (hypothesis): The polynomial-limit lemma requires uniformly bounded degrees and a nonzero coefficientwise limit. Ordered-root continuity additionally concerns monic real-rooted polynomials of fixed degree. Blocked use: ['Apply the lemma to a zero limiting polynomial.', 'Transfer ordered-root continuity to variable-degree or nonmonic families without further argument.']
- **limit-barrier-domain-and-shift** (hypothesis): The barrier shift requires real stability, a point above the roots, and the stated barrier inequality for a positive shift. Its conclusion does not apply at an arbitrary evaluation point. Blocked use: ['Evaluate logarithmic barriers at zeros and apply the shift rule.', 'Shift coordinates without checking the barrier hypothesis.']
- **limit-dual-supremum-and-slack** (nonconstructive): The dual comparison concerns optimization values over positive-energy binary embeddings and row semiorthogonal matrices. Feasible shortcut matrices are obtained with strict slack; attainment of the shortcut infimum is not asserted. Blocked use: ['Treat the comparison as an identity for an arbitrary embedding.', 'Assume an optimizer exists at the exact shortcut infimum.', 'Assert an exact finite binary representation of irrational cut weights.']
- **limit-spectral-packing-positive-signal** (hypothesis): The spectral packing estimate requires a nonempty edge set and a positive diagonal-coordinate signal satisfying the stated energy comparison. Blocked use: ['Demand separated endpoint centers when all endpoint images coincide.', 'Invoke the positive-radius packing conclusion after dropping the positive-signal assumption.']
- **limit-bag-extraction-parameter-regime** (hypothesis): Geometric bag extraction requires positive boundary sums at bad nodes and the stated smallness and parameter conditions. The resulting sequence consists entirely of compact bags or entirely of assigned bags. Blocked use: ['Mix the charging hypotheses of the compact and assigned alternatives.', 'Omit the smallness condition while retaining the mass guarantee.']
- **limit-compact-charging-separation** (hypothesis): Compact-bag charging requires the prescribed compactness and geometric scale separation, together with disjoint balls within each family. It bounds bag mass by graph length, rather than graph length from above. Blocked use: ['Apply compact charging to overlapping balls within a family.', 'Reverse the charging inequality to obtain an upper bound on total edge length.']
- **limit-shell-witnesses** (hypothesis): The disjoint-shell length bound requires both an inner vertex witness and an outer vertex witness for every shell, as well as pairwise geometric disjointness. Blocked use: ['Charge an arbitrary shell having no vertices witnessing a proper radial cut.', 'Sum widths of overlapping shells using the disjoint-shell lemma alone.']
- **limit-assigned-pruning-and-labels** (hypothesis): Assigned-bag charging requires local hierarchy connectivity, the selected-node boundary ratio, sufficient assignment, and separated scales. Noninsertable balls are pruned before the insertable-arrangement argument applies. Blocked use: ['Apply the insertable-arrangement lemma to unpruned bags without proving insertability.', 'Drop predecessor boundary checks or the selected-node boundary ratio.']
- **limit-labeled-routing-separation** (formal_scope): Geometrically overlapping records can be charged only through the valid-labeling conditions, including disjoint conflict domains and crossing-path witnesses. Radial restrictions inherit individual validity when they retain labels and have positive width. Blocked use: ['Charge entire witness-path edges repeatedly instead of disjoint edge portions.', 'Change record labels and assume crossing-path validity follows merely from geometric containment.']
- **limit-interior-and-phase-accounting** (hypothesis): Interior processing preserves existing allocations under its valid-arrangement assumptions. The shrink-deficit estimate applies under the small-excess condition, so it cannot replace the separate phase-transition accounting unconditionally. Blocked use: ['Shrink old records without checking the available border credit.', 'Use the shrink-deficit bound outside its small-excess regime.']
- **limit-iteration-stopping-rule** (formal_scope): Sparsification stops when the packing parameter falls below the absolute threshold. Small initial packing cases use an arbitrary spanning tree with the resulting weaker constant bound. Blocked use: ['Continue applying the large-packing proposition after its precondition fails.', 'Claim that an arbitrary initial spanning tree already achieves the main inverse-connectivity guarantee with the same argument.']
- **limit-fixed-point-exact-existence** (nonconstructive): The fixed-point theorem requires a nonempty compact convex finite-dimensional domain and a continuous self-map. Its proof obtains exact existence through a compactness limit rather than a finite exact stopping certificate. Blocked use: ['Apply the theorem to a discontinuous response map.', 'Infer a finite exact fixed-point algorithm from successive triangulations alone.']

Suppose that $a>0$ and $$\\label{hb:spectral-assumption}\n \\Upsilon:=\\left(\\frac1m\\sum_{e\\in F}Y_{e,e}\\right)^2>0,\n \\qquad\n \\Upsilon\\ge\\frac a m\\sum_{e\\in F}\\|Y_e\\|_2^2.$$ Then $a\\le1$, and there are $b\\ge2$ pairwise disjoint Euclidean balls of one positive squared radius $r$, centered at images of endpoints of edges in $F$, such that $$\\label{hb:spectral-logarithmic}\n b\\ge\\frac{am}{16},\n \\qquad\n br\\ge\\frac{9m\\Upsilon}\n {512\\bigl(5+\\log_2(1/a)\\bigr)^2}.$$ Consequently, for every $0<\\varepsilon<1/3$, the choice $$\\label{hb:spectral-constant}\n C_1(\\varepsilon)=\\frac{1024}{\\varepsilon^2}$$ gives $$\\label{hb:spectral-conclusion}\n b\\ge\\frac{a|F|}{C_1(\\varepsilon)},\n \\qquad\n br\\ge\\frac{a^\\varepsilon\\Upsilon|F|}{C_1(\\varepsilon)}.$$",

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 174 (`lean/docs/174.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [The strong thin tree conjecture](../../preprints/The-strong-thin-tree-conjecture-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A polynomial-time construction of strong thin trees](../../preprints/A-polynomial-time-construction-of-strong-thin-trees-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The strong thin-tree conjecture asks for spanning trees that cross every cut sparsely relative to the original graph. The formalized result gives one absolute constant $C>0$ such that every finite loopless $k$-edge-connected multigraph with at least two vertices has a spanning tree crossing each nontrivial cut at most $(C/k)$ times the original cut size. Parallel edges remain distinct. No numerical value of $C$ or construction algorithm is asserted.
>
> The strong thin-tree problem asks for a spanning tree that crosses every cut sparsely relative to the original graph. The formalization gives one deterministic polynomial-time algorithm and an absolute constant $C>0$ such that a finite $k$-edge-connected loopless multigraph yields a spanning tree using at most a $C/k$ fraction of the edges of every cut. The input may encode parallel-edge multiplicities in binary, and the running time is polynomial in that binary input length.

Comparator source `StrongThinTree.lean` (1358 bytes, sha256 `f8ca2f8c8078052bbd345b09087ad6c821d4a1db62fbaa163604e03d8c745980`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace StrongThinTree

structure MultiGraph (n m : ℕ) where
  left : Fin m → Fin n
  right : Fin m → Fin n
  loopless : ∀ e, left e ≠ right e

namespace MultiGraph

def cut {n m : ℕ} (G : MultiGraph n m) (T : Finset (Fin m))
    (S : Finset (Fin n)) : Finset (Fin m) :=
  T.filter fun e => (G.left e ∈ S ∧ G.right e ∉ S) ∨
    (G.right e ∈ S ∧ G.left e ∉ S)

def Connected {n m : ℕ} (G : MultiGraph n m) (T : Finset (Fin m)) : Prop :=
  ∀ S : Finset (Fin n), S.Nonempty → S ≠ Finset.univ → (G.cut T S).Nonempty

def SpanningTree {n m : ℕ} (G : MultiGraph n m) (T : Finset (Fin m)) : Prop :=
  G.Connected T ∧ ∀ e ∈ T, ¬ G.Connected (T.erase e)

def EdgeConnected {n m : ℕ} (G : MultiGraph n m) (k : ℕ) : Prop :=
  ∀ S : Finset (Fin n), S.Nonempty → S ≠ Finset.univ →
    k ≤ (G.cut Finset.univ S).card

end MultiGraph

def MainStatement : Prop :=
  ∃ C : ℝ, 0 < C ∧ ∀ (k : ℕ), 1 ≤ k → ∀ (n m : ℕ), 2 ≤ n →
    ∀ G : MultiGraph n m, G.EdgeConnected k →
      ∃ T : Finset (Fin m), G.SpanningTree T ∧
        ∀ S : Finset (Fin n), S.Nonempty → S ≠ Finset.univ →
          ((G.cut T S).card : ℝ) ≤ (C / (k : ℝ)) * (G.cut Finset.univ S).card

theorem strongThinTree : MainStatement := by
  sorry

end StrongThinTree

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that every finite loopless <i>k</i>-edge-connected multigraph on at least two vertices, with <i>k</i> ≥ 1, has a spanning tree meeting each cut in at most $`C/k`$ times the size of the cut, where <i>C</i> is a universal constant. This resolves the strong thin tree conjecture.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

For a finite loopless undirected multigraph $G=(V,E)$ and a nonempty proper subset $S\subset V$, write $\delta_G(S)$ for the edges with exactly one endpoint in $S$. Parallel edges are counted separately. A graph is $k$-edge-connected if $|\delta_G(S)|\ge k$ for every nonempty proper $S$. A spanning tree $T$ is $\alpha$-thin if $$|\delta_T(S)|\le\alpha|\delta_G(S)|
 \qquad(\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq V).$$ A spanning tree retains connectivity using only $|V|-1$ edges. Thinness asks that these edges occupy a small fraction of every cut simultaneously. The question is whether high edge connectivity alone forces such a tree, with a bound independent of the number of vertices.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a universal constant $C>0$ such that, for every integer $k\ge1$, every finite $k$-edge-connected undirected multigraph without loops and with at least two vertices has a spanning tree $T$ satisfying $$|\delta_T(S)|\le\frac Ck\,|\delta_G(S)|
 \qquad\text{for every }\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq V.$$*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The conjecture and its history

Goddyn’s thin tree conjecture asks whether, for every $\varepsilon>0$, a sufficiently high edge connectivity guarantees an $\varepsilon$-thin spanning tree (Goddyn 2004) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Goddyn](#ref-Goddyn)]. The strong thin tree conjecture asks for the quantitative rate $C/k$. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves this stronger conjecture positively. The order $1/k$ is necessary: with two vertices and $k$ parallel edges, every spanning tree uses exactly one of the $k$ edges of the only cut.

Thin trees connect this graph-theoretic question with approximation algorithms. In the asymmetric traveling salesman problem, one seeks a minimum-cost tour through all vertices of a directed metric. Asadpour, Goemans, Mądry, Oveis Gharan, and Saberi used maximum-entropy distributions on spanning trees to round the standard linear-programming relaxation, obtaining an $O(\log n/\log\log n)$-approximation, where $n$ is the number of vertices (Asadpour et al. 2010, 2017) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[AsadpourSODA](#ref-AsadpourSODA), [Asadpour](#ref-Asadpour)]. Their method explains the usefulness of controlling every cut: together with control of the tree’s cost, cut bounds permit an inexpensive Eulerian augmentation of a suitable oriented tree. For the underlying thin-tree problem, Oveis Gharan and Saberi established the inverse-connectivity bound for planar graphs and, with constants depending on the surface, for graphs of bounded orientable genus (Oveis Gharan and Saberi 2011, Theorem 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[OveisGharanSaberi2011](#ref-OveisGharanSaberi2011)].

For general multigraphs, Anari and Oveis Gharan developed effective-resistance-reducing flows and the geometry of locally connected hierarchies. They proved the existence of a spanning tree with thinness $\operatorname{poly}(\log\log n)/k$ (Anari and Oveis Gharan 2015, Corollary 1.8) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[AOG](#ref-AOG)]. Their work provides the principal geometric foundation of the present proof. Progress for specified families of cuts gives a complementary view of the problem. Klein and Olver obtained inverse-connectivity thinness for every prescribed laminar family (Klein and Olver 2023) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[KleinOlver2023](#ref-KleinOlver2023)]. Klein, Olver, and Yeoh constructed a spanning tree crossing every cut of size less than $(1+1/40)k$ at most $88$ times (Klein, Olver, and Yeoh 2026, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[KleinOlverYeoh](#ref-KleinOlverYeoh)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> controls all cuts simultaneously and removes the dependence on $n$.

The thin-tree approach to asymmetric traveling salesman remains structurally useful, although constant-factor approximation for that problem has already been obtained by other methods. Svensson, Tarnawski, and Végh proved the first such guarantee (Svensson, Tarnawski, and Végh 2020) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[SvenssonTarnawskiVegh2020](#ref-SvenssonTarnawskiVegh2020)]; Traub and Vygen subsequently improved it (Traub and Vygen 2022) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[TraubVygen2022](#ref-TraubVygen2022)]. Our objective here is the underlying all-cut spanning-tree theorem.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The two main deductions

High edge connectivity supplies many edge-disjoint spanning trees through the Nash–Williams–Tutte theorem (Nash-Williams 1961; Tutte 1961) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[NashWilliams](#ref-NashWilliams), [Tutte](#ref-Tutte)]. For any fixed cut, the average number of edges used by the trees in such a packing is small. This averaging does not select one tree that is small on every cut. We maintain a large packing throughout a sequence of sparsifications: each step reduces cut sizes and the packing size by almost the same factor, and the accumulated relative loss remains bounded.

The first deduction extracts a large tree packing on edges of small resistance. For a graph $H$, let $b_e=\mathbf1_u-\mathbf1_v$ be an arbitrarily oriented incidence column of an edge $e=uv$, and let $L_H=\sum_e b_eb_e^{\mathsf T}$ be its Laplacian. The resistance of $e$ with respect to a positive definite matrix $X$ is $b_e^{\mathsf T}X^{-1}b_e$. Write $\mathbf1_S$ for the indicator column of $S$, and $A\preceq B$ when $B-A$ is positive semidefinite. For all sufficiently large integers $r$, if $H$ on at least two vertices contains $r$ edge-disjoint spanning trees, we construct such a matrix $X$ with $$L_H\preceq X,\qquad
 \mathbf1_S^{\mathsf T}X\mathbf1_S
 \le3|\delta_H(S)|
 \quad(\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq V),$$ such that the edges of $H$ with $b_e^{\mathsf T}X^{-1}b_e\le4r^{-1/2}$ still contain $\lfloor(1-r^{-1/16})r\rfloor$ edge-disjoint spanning trees. The starting point is a polynomial consequence of the locally connected hierarchy theorem of Anari and Oveis Gharan (Anari and Oveis Gharan 2015, Theorem 3.5) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[AOG](#ref-AOG)]; we prove the required consequence in full. For a given matrix, retain the edges below each resistance threshold and partition the vertices into maximal sets supporting the prescribed number of disjoint spanning trees. These partitions are nested. Selecting them by dyadic crossing-edge counts gives a rooted hierarchy of nested vertex sets and avoids a loss depending on the number of levels. The hierarchy estimate supplies a response matrix $Y$ with small resistance on almost all crossing edges of every selected partition at once (Lemma <a href="#lem:response" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:response">9</a>). We interpolate finitely many such responses and take a fixed-point limit. At the resulting matrix $X$, a nontrivial maximal packing partition of its own low-resistance graph would have too many crossing edges. This contradiction yields the extracted packing (Theorem <a href="#thm:extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:extraction">8</a>).

The second deduction is a sparsification step that retains about half the tree packing while bounding each new cut by approximately half its previous size. We use two vector representations of the incidence columns. The matrix $X$ controls cut size. A separate isotropic representation—whose vector outer products sum to the identity— controls the partition inequalities for tree packing. Applying the Marcus–Spielman–Srivastava theorem (Marcus, Spielman, and Srivastava 2015) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[MSS](#ref-MSS)] to the stacked columns controls both systems on the same selected edge set. The relative losses are $1+O(r^{-1/16})$. They have a bounded product as the packing parameter decreases geometrically. Harvey and Olver used iterated matrix halving to obtain spectrally thin trees under an effective-resistance hypothesis (Harvey and Olver 2014, Appendix F) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[HarveyOlver2014](#ref-HarveyOlver2014)]. The additional packing representation here permits iteration from edge connectivity.

Preserving a packing requires more than a weighted spectral approximation. Sampling by effective resistance and spectral sparsification provide powerful ways to reduce a graph while approximately preserving its Laplacian (Spielman and Srivastava 2011; Batson, Spielman, and Srivastava 2012) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[SpielmanSrivastava2011](#ref-SpielmanSrivastava2011), [BatsonSpielmanSrivastava2012](#ref-BatsonSpielmanSrivastava2012)]. Here the edges must retain their original unit multiplicities. For example, $q$ parallel edges between two vertices have the same Laplacian as one edge of weight $q$, but the former graph packs $q$ edge-disjoint spanning trees and the latter has only one. The second representation supplies the rank information needed to certify the partition inequalities after selecting an unweighted subset of edges.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Organization and inputs

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> proves the tree-packing facts used below. Section <a href="#sec:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:hierarchy">3</a> gives the precise hierarchy input, and Section <a href="#sec:extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:extraction">4</a> proves the extraction theorem. Sections <a href="#sec:auxiliary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:auxiliary">5</a> and <a href="#sec:sparsification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sparsification">6</a> develop the auxiliary representation and the sparsification step. Section <a href="#sec:iteration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:iteration">7</a> proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, including the small-connectivity cases. The independent-vector theorem used in the sparsification appears as Theorem <a href="#thm:mss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mss">15</a>. The required hierarchy estimate and the finite-dimensional fixed-point theorem are proved in the appendices. All sufficiently large thresholds in the argument are absolute. Choices made after a particular finite graph has been fixed need not have a uniform algorithmic running time. The companion article *A polynomial-time construction of strong thin trees* (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[ConstructiveThinTreesCompanion](#ref-ConstructiveThinTreesCompanion)] develops the separate finite-bit construction, including graphs given by binary-encoded parallel-edge multiplicities. Its geometric input extends the hierarchy proof here from cut tests to a larger cone of metric tests.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Laplacians and tree packings

Unless a base is specified, $\log$ denotes the natural logarithm.

All graphs in the proof are finite undirected loopless multigraphs. They have a common vertex set when one is said to be a subgraph of another. Each parallel edge has its own index. For a partition $\mathcal P$ of the vertex set, let $E_H(\mathcal P)$ denote the edges whose endpoints lie in different parts. A graph is $r$-tree-packable if it contains $r$ pairwise edge-disjoint spanning trees. On a singleton vertex set this property is taken to hold for every positive integer $r$.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Matrix conventions

Choose an orientation of each edge solely to define $b_e=\mathbf1_u-\mathbf1_v\in\mathbb R^V$, and put $$L_H=\sum_{e\in E(H)}b_eb_e^{\mathsf T}.$$ Changing an orientation changes no matrix or resistance used below. For a real symmetric positive definite matrix $X$, define $$R_X(e)=b_e^{\mathsf T}X^{-1}b_e.$$ The order $A\preceq B$ means that $B-A$ is positive semidefinite. We use the operator norm for matrices unless otherwise specified. For every nonempty proper $S\subset V$, $$\mathbf1_S^{\mathsf T}L_H\mathbf1_S=|\delta_H(S)|.$$ An inequality required only for these indicator vectors is called a *cut inequality*. It need not be a semidefinite inequality. In particular, no cut inequality is imposed at $S=V$.

<a id="stmt-lem-resistance-order"></a>
<div id="lem:resistance-order" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2**. *For $X\succ0$, a column $b$, and $t>0$, $$b^{\mathsf T}X^{-1}b\le t
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 bb^{\mathsf T}\preceq tX.$$ If $X\succeq Y\succ0$, then $b^{\mathsf T}X^{-1}b\le b^{\mathsf T}Y^{-1}b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-resistance-order"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-resistance-order</summary>

*Proof.* Congruence by $X^{-1/2}$ reduces the first assertion to the fact that the only possible nonzero eigenvalue of $aa^{\mathsf T}$ is $\|a\|^2$, with $a=X^{-1/2}b$. For the second, $Y^{-1/2}XY^{-1/2}\succeq I$; diagonalization and inversion give $X^{-1}\preceq Y^{-1}$. ◻

</details>

When a Laplacian is inverted, we will explicitly restrict it to $$\mathbf1^\perp=\{x\in\mathbb R^V:\textstyle\sum_{v\in V}x_v=0\}.$$ The shortcut matrices $X$ used in extraction are positive definite on the full space $\mathbb R^V$, so their inverses require no such convention.

<a id="stmt-lem-incidence-rank"></a>
<div id="lem:incidence-rank" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Incidence rank). *If $F$ has $n$ vertices and $c$ connected components, its incidence columns span a space of dimension $n-c$. In particular the columns of a spanning tree form a basis of $\mathbf1^\perp$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-incidence-rank"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-incidence-rank</summary>

*Proof.* A vector is orthogonal to every incidence column precisely when its coordinates agree at the endpoints of each edge, equivalently when it is constant on every connected component. The orthogonal complement thus has dimension $c$. A tree on $n$ vertices has $n-1$ edges, as follows by deleting leaves successively, so its spanning columns form a basis. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The partition criterion

<a id="stmt-thm-packing"></a>
<div id="thm:packing" class="theorem">

**Theorem 4** (Nash–Williams–Tutte). *For a positive integer $r$, a graph $H$ contains $r$ edge-disjoint spanning trees if and only if $$|E_H(\mathcal P)|\ge r(|\mathcal P|-1)
 \qquad\text{for every partition }\mathcal P\text{ of }V(H).$$*

</div>

We include an exchange proof of this classical criterion (Nash-Williams 1961; Tutte 1961) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[NashWilliams](#ref-NashWilliams), [Tutte](#ref-Tutte)]. The insertion and cycle exchanges below are the graphic form of the circuit exchanges in Edmonds’s matroid-partition proof (Edmonds 1965, sec. 1.6) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Edmonds1965](#ref-Edmonds1965)].

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-packing"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-packing</summary>

*Proof.* The necessity follows by counting the crossings of each tree. For sufficiency, choose $r$ disjoint forests whose total number $m$ of edges is maximum. A *state* is such a choice. Starting from this state, allow a move that adds an unused edge to one forest and removes an edge of the resulting cycle.

In every reachable state, every unused edge closes a cycle with each forest: otherwise adding it would increase $m$. Let $U$ be the set of edges that are unused in at least one reachable state. If an unused edge is added to a forest, every edge of the resulting cycle lies in $U$, since any one of its original edges can be made unused by the corresponding move. Such a move preserves the components of that forest restricted to its edges in $U$. It also leaves the other forests unchanged.

Fix $e\in U$. In a state where $e$ is unused, its endpoints are connected by edges in $U$ in each forest. The component invariance just proved carries this statement to the initial state. Consequently each component of $(V(H),U)$ is spanned by the $U$-edges of every forest. If there are $d$ such components, this accounts for $r(n-d)$ used edges, where $n=|V(H)|$. Every edge between these components is used, since every unused edge belongs to $U$. Their number is therefore at most $$m-r(n-d).$$ If $m<r(n-1)$, this is smaller than $r(d-1)$, contrary to the partition hypothesis. Thus $m=r(n-1)$; each of the $r$ forests has $n-1$ edges and is a spanning tree. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-packing-parts"></a>
<div id="lem:packing-parts" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Union and quotient properties). *Fix a positive integer $q$ and a graph $F$.*

1.  *If $F[A]$ and $F[B]$ are $q$-tree-packable and $A\cap B\ne\varnothing$, then $F[A\cup B]$ is $q$-tree-packable.*

2.  *The inclusion-maximal $q$-tree-packable vertex sets form a partition $\mathcal P(F)$. If $F\subseteq F'$, then $\mathcal P(F')$ coarsens $\mathcal P(F)$.*

3.  *If $|\mathcal P(F)|>1$, then $$|E_F(\mathcal P(F))|<q(|\mathcal P(F)|-1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-packing-parts"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-packing-parts</summary>

*Proof.* For (i), enumerate the $q$ trees on each of $A$ and $B$. In the $i$-th tree on $B$, contract all vertices of $A\cap B$ to a single vertex, discard loops, and choose a spanning tree of the resulting connected multigraph. Its selected edges, together with the $i$-th tree on $A$, form a tree on $A\cup B$. Every selected edge has an endpoint outside $A$, so no such edge lies in any tree on $A$. The selections in different colors remain disjoint. This proves (i).

Every singleton is packable, and finiteness gives maximal sets. Part (i) shows that two maximal sets cannot intersect unless equal. Every previously packable set remains packable when edges are added, which proves (ii).

For (iii), contract the parts and remove loops. If its crossing count were at least $q(d-1)$, where $d=|\mathcal P(F)|>1$, the quotient would have a set $W$ of at least two vertices with $|E(W)|\ge q(|W|-1)$. Choose such $W$ inclusion-minimal. For each proper nonempty $J\subset W$, $$|E(J)|\le q(|J|-1);$$ this also holds for a singleton because loops were removed. For a partition $W=J_1\sqcup\cdots\sqcup J_t$, with $t\ge2$, subtraction gives at least $$q(|W|-1)-\sum_{\ell=1}^t q(|J_\ell|-1)=q(t-1)$$ edges between its parts. Theorem <a href="#thm:packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:packing">4</a> supplies $q$ quotient trees on $W$. Attach to each color a packed tree inside each of its quotient vertices. This gives $q$ disjoint spanning trees on their union in $F$, contradicting maximality of the parts. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:hierarchy"></a>
### Shortcuts for a locally connected hierarchy

We require one matrix that controls average resistances on several related boundaries. A hierarchy specifies the boundaries on which this control is required. The matrix need only obey an upper bound on cuts; an upper bound in the positive semidefinite order would be a stronger condition.

<a id="stmt-def-hierarchy"></a>
<div id="def:hierarchy" class="definition">

**Definition 6** (Hierarchy and its boundaries). Let $H=(V,E)$ be a finite loopless multigraph. A *hierarchy* on $V$ is a finite rooted tree of distinct nonempty vertex sets. Its root is $V$, its leaves are the singleton sets, and the children of every nonleaf form a partition of that node into at least two sets. For a nonroot node $A$, write $A^*$ for its parent and define $$P(A)=\delta_H(A),\qquad
 O(A)=E_H(A,A^*\setminus A).$$ All edge sets here retain multiplicities. Given an integer $h\ge2$, the hierarchy is *locally $h$-connected* if $H[A]$ is $h$-edge-connected for every node $A$. For singleton nodes this condition is vacuous.

</div>

In this definition $|O(A)|\ge h$ for every nonroot $A$: it is the boundary of the nonempty proper set $A$ in the $h$-edge-connected graph $H[A^*]$. An edge belongs to $O(A)$ for exactly two nodes of the full hierarchy. Indeed, if $C$ is the least node containing both endpoints, these two nodes are the two children of $C$ containing its endpoints. Consequently an edge belongs to at most two parent boundaries in any subcollection.

<a id="eq:hierarchy-shortcut"></a>
Orient each edge arbitrarily and let $b_e$ be its incidence column. As usual, $L_H=\sum_{e\in E}b_eb_e^{\mathsf T}$. A *shortcut matrix* for $H$ means a symmetric positive definite matrix $D$ on $\mathbb R^V$ such that $$\label{eq:hierarchy-shortcut}
 \mathbf 1_S^{\mathsf T}D\mathbf 1_S\le |\delta_H(S)|
 \qquad(\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq V).$$ Its edge resistance is $R_D(e)=b_e^{\mathsf T}D^{-1}b_e$. The restriction to proper nonempty cuts in <a href="#eq:hierarchy-shortcut" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-shortcut">[eq:hierarchy-shortcut]</a> is essential: imposing the same inequality at $S=V$ would contradict positive definiteness. We do not impose $D\preceq L_H$.

The following estimate is the hierarchy input used in this paper. It is a polynomial consequence of the Tree-CP theorem of Anari and Oveis Gharan (Anari and Oveis Gharan 2015, Theorem 3.5, printed p. 22) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[AOG](#ref-AOG)]. We give a complete proof in Appendix <a href="#app:hierarchy-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:hierarchy-proof">9</a>, following their dual and geometric method with parameters chosen for this weaker bound.

<a id="stmt-thm-hierarchy-power"></a>
<div id="thm:hierarchy-power" class="theorem">

**Theorem 7** (Polynomial shortcut bound). *There is an absolute integer $q_H\ge2$ such that the following holds for each integer $q\ge q_H$. Let $H$ have a locally $q$-connected hierarchy, and let $\mathcal M$ be any collection of its nonroot nodes satisfying $|O(A)|\ge |P(A)|/q$. Then $H$ has a shortcut matrix $D$ such that <a id="eq:hierarchy-power"></a>$$\label{eq:hierarchy-power}
 \frac{1}{|O(A)|}\sum_{e\in O(A)}R_D(e)\le q^{-31/40}
 \qquad(A\in\mathcal M).$$ The cutoff is independent of the graph, the hierarchy depth and the marked collection $\mathcal M$.*

</div>

<a id="sec:extraction"></a>
### Extracting a tree packing with small resistance

Throughout this section, $H$ is an $r$-tree-packable graph on $n\ge2$ vertices. Set <a id="eq:parameters"></a>$$\label{eq:parameters}
 u=r^{-1/16},\qquad q=\lfloor(1-u)r\rfloor.$$ We take $r$ above an absolute threshold, so in particular $q>1$. The threshold may be increased finitely many times below.

<a id="stmt-thm-extraction"></a>
<div id="thm:extraction" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:shortcut-X"></a>
**Theorem 8** (Shortcut extraction). *For all sufficiently large integers $r$, every $r$-tree-packable graph $H$ admits $X\succ0$ such that $$\label{eq:shortcut-X}
 L_H\preceq X,\qquad
 \mathbf1_S^{\mathsf T}X\mathbf1_S\le3|\delta_H(S)|
 \quad(\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq V),$$ and the edges with $R_X(e)\le4r^{-1/2}$ contain $q$ edge-disjoint spanning trees. The lower threshold on $r$ is independent of $H$ and $n$.*

</div>

For a nontrivial maximal packing partition, Lemma <a href="#lem:packing-parts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:packing-parts">5</a>(iii) limits the number of crossing edges in the threshold graph. We first construct a response that makes most crossing edges have small resistance. Averaging responses at nearby matrices and taking a fixed-point limit transfers this estimate to the matrix defining the partition, forcing that partition to be trivial.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### A compact set of matrices

Let <a id="eq:compact-domain"></a>$$\label{eq:compact-domain}
 \mathcal K=
 \left\{X=X^{\mathsf T}:
 X\succeq L_H+n^{-1}I,\quad
 \mathbf1_S^{\mathsf T}X\mathbf1_S\le3|\delta_H(S)|
 \text{ for }\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq V\right\}.$$ This is a nonempty compact convex subset of the real symmetric matrices. Indeed, $H$ is connected, and $|S|/n<1\le|\delta_H(S)|$, so $L_H+n^{-1}I$ belongs to $\mathcal K$. Closedness and convexity follow from the defining inequalities. The singleton cuts give $X_{vv}\le3\deg_H(v)$, and positive semidefiniteness gives $|X_{vw}|^2\le X_{vv}X_{ww}$; hence the domain is bounded. Every matrix in $\mathcal K$ is bounded below by $n^{-1}I$.

<a id="eq:hierarchy-response"></a>
<a id="eq:response-average"></a>
Given any locally $q$-connected hierarchy in $H$, mark exactly the nonroot nodes satisfying $$|O(A)|\ge q^{-1}|P(A)|.$$ For all sufficiently large absolute $r$, we have $q\ge r/2$ and $q\ge q_H$. Theorem <a href="#thm:hierarchy-power" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:hierarchy-power">7</a> gives a shortcut matrix $D$ whose average resistance on every marked $O(A)$ is at most $$q^{-31/40}\le2^{31/40}r^{-31/40}\le r^{-3/4},$$ where the final inequality holds for $r\ge2^{31}$. Put $$\label{eq:hierarchy-response}
 Y=L_H+n^{-1}I+D.$$ Then $Y\in\mathcal K$, since on every cut its quadratic form is at most $2|\delta_H(S)|+|S|/n\le3|\delta_H(S)|$. Lemma <a href="#lem:resistance-order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:resistance-order">2</a> also gives $$\label{eq:response-average}
 \frac1{|O(A)|}\sum_{e\in O(A)}R_Y(e)\le r^{-3/4}
 \qquad\text{for every marked }A.$$

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Packing partitions and a simultaneous response

Fix $X\in\mathcal K$. For $s>0$, let $$F_s(X)=\bigl(V,\{e\in E(H):R_X(e)\le s\}\bigr),
 \qquad
 \mathcal P_s(X)=\mathcal P(F_s(X)),$$ where $\mathcal P(F)$ is the maximal packing partition of Lemma <a href="#lem:packing-parts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:packing-parts">5</a>. Define $$B_s(X)=E_H(\mathcal P_s(X)),\qquad M_s(X)=|B_s(X)|.$$ The crossing sets contain edges of $H$, including those absent from $F_s(X)$. As $s$ increases, the partitions coarsen and their crossing sets decrease. Only finitely many partitions occur. Each part induces a $q$-edge-connected subgraph of $H$, because it contains $q$ packed trees in $F_s(X)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-response"></a>
<div id="lem:response" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:simultaneous-response"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Simultaneous response). *There is an absolute constant $C_0$, which may be taken to be $40$, such that for every $X\in\mathcal K$ there exists $Y\in\mathcal K$ with $$\label{eq:simultaneous-response}
 \bigl|\{e\in B_s(X):R_Y(e)>r^{-1/2}\}\bigr|
 \le C_0r^{-1/4}M_s(X)
 \qquad\text{for every }s>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-response"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-response</summary>

*Proof.* Temporarily suppress $X$ from the notation. In each occupied bin $2^a\le M_s<2^{a+1}$, $a\ge0$, choose the finest partition that occurs in that bin. List the chosen partitions in order from fine to coarse as $\mathcal P_0,\ldots,\mathcal P_{\ell-1}$, with crossing sets $B_i$ and sizes $M_i$. Append $\mathcal P_\ell=\{V\}$, $B_\ell=\varnothing$, $M_\ell=0$.

The distinct parts of these partitions, together with all singletons, form a laminar family containing $V$. Give each proper set its smallest proper containing set as parent. The children of every nonsingleton partition that set, so this is a hierarchy. Every induced graph at a node is $q$-edge-connected: this was proved for the partition parts, the root is $r$-tree-packable, and singleton conditions are vacuous. Take the response $Y$ from <a href="#eq:hierarchy-response" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hierarchy-response">[eq:hierarchy-response]</a> for this hierarchy.

Consider a transition $\mathcal P_j$ to $\mathcal P_{j+1}$. If a part $A\in\mathcal P_j$ changes, the part of $\mathcal P_{j+1}$ containing it is its immediate parent in the hierarchy. Indeed, earlier selected partitions are finer and later ones are coarser, so none supplies a distinct set strictly between these two. Adding singleton leaves creates no such set.

An edge of $B_j\setminus B_{j+1}$ has endpoints in two changing parts of $\mathcal P_j$ with the same parent. It belongs to the $O(A)$ set of each of those parts; see Figure <a href="#fig:hierarchy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:hierarchy">1</a>. The total full boundary size of all changing parts is at most $2M_j$, since they belong to the single partition $\mathcal P_j$. For the unmarked changing parts, $$\sum_A |O(A)|\le \frac1q\sum_A|P(A)|\le\frac{2M_j}{q}.$$ For the marked changing parts, <a href="#eq:response-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:response-average">[eq:response-average]</a> gives $$\bigl|\{e\in O(A):R_Y(e)>r^{-1/2}\}\bigr|
 \le r^{-1/4}|O(A)|.$$ Their total bad-edge count is therefore at most $2M_jr^{-1/4}$. These counts may cover an edge more than once, which only enlarges the upper bound. Consequently at most $$2(r^{-1/4}+1/q)M_j$$ of the disappearing edges are bad.

There is only one representative per occupied dyadic bin, so its bin exponents strictly decrease. For every $i<\ell$, <a id="eq:dyadic-tail"></a>$$\label{eq:dyadic-tail}
 \sum_{j=i}^{\ell-1}M_j<4M_i.$$ For example, if $2^a\le M_i<2^{a+1}$, the successive terms are bounded by $2^{a+1},2^a,2^{a-1},\ldots$. Every edge of $B_i$ disappears at exactly one later transition. Its bad-edge count is thus at most $$8(r^{-1/4}+1/q)M_i.$$ For an arbitrary $s$ with $M_s>0$, the chosen representative in its bin is finer, so $B_s\subseteq B_i$ and $M_i<2M_s$. The bad-edge count in $B_s$ is at most $$16(r^{-1/4}+1/q)M_s\le40r^{-1/4}M_s$$ for all sufficiently large $r$. If $M_s=0$, the assertion is immediate. This proves <a href="#eq:simultaneous-response" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:simultaneous-response">[eq:simultaneous-response]</a> simultaneously for every threshold. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:hierarchy">

<figcaption>At a selected transition $𝒫_{i} → 𝒫_{i + 1}$, the parts $A, A′$ merge into their immediate parent $C$. The edge $e$ leaves the crossing set and belongs to both $O(A)$ and $O(A′)$, whereas $f$ remains a crossing edge. Only representative vertices and edges are shown.</figcaption>
</figure>

    - Box $A$ containing two vertices (dots).

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### A consistent matrix from a fixed-point limit

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-response-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-response</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:extraction">8</a>.* The response supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:response" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:response">9</a> need not vary continuously with $X$. We interpolate only finitely many responses at a time.

For each positive integer $p$, cover $\mathcal K$ by finitely many relative open balls of radius $1/p$, with centers $C_{p,1},\ldots,C_{p,N_p}\in\mathcal K$. At each center choose a response $Y_{p,j}$. Define continuous weights $$\phi_{p,j}(Z)=
 \frac{\max\{0,p^{-1}-\|Z-C_{p,j}\|\}}
 {\sum_{h=1}^{N_p}\max\{0,p^{-1}-\|Z-C_{p,h}\|\}}
 \qquad(Z\in\mathcal K).$$ The denominator is positive by the open cover. The map $$\Phi_p(Z)=\sum_{j=1}^{N_p}\phi_{p,j}(Z)Y_{p,j}$$ is a continuous self-map of the compact convex set $\mathcal K$. Theorem <a href="#thm:fixedpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:fixedpoint">46</a> gives $X_p=\Phi_p(X_p)$. Pass to a subsequence along which $X_p\to X\in\mathcal K$.

Choose $$3r^{-1/2}<s<4r^{-1/2}$$ distinct from every $R_X(e)$, $e\in E(H)$. This is possible because the graph is finite. If $\phi_{p,j}(X_p)>0$, then $$\|C_{p,j}-X\|<p^{-1}+\|X_p-X\|\longrightarrow0$$ uniformly over these centers. The resistance of each edge is continuous on $\mathcal K$. Since all resistances at $X$ avoid $s$, for all sufficiently large $p$ every contributing center has exactly the same threshold graph, packing partition, and crossing set as $X$.

Write $B=B_s(X)$, $M=|B|$, and $\delta=C_0r^{-1/4}$. If $M=0$, connectedness of $H$ implies that its partition is $\{V\}$, and the conclusion already follows. Otherwise, for large $p$, each contributing $Y_{p,j}$ has at most $\delta M$ bad edges in this same set $B$. Summing their bad indicators with weights $\phi_{p,j}(X_p)$ shows that at most $2\delta M$ edges receive bad weight greater than one half.

For every other edge $e\in B$, at least half the total weight is on matrices satisfying $$Y_{p,j}\succeq r^{1/2}b_eb_e^{\mathsf T},$$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:resistance-order" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:resistance-order">2</a>. All remaining matrices are positive semidefinite. Hence their convex combination satisfies $$X_p\succeq\tfrac12r^{1/2}b_eb_e^{\mathsf T},
 \qquad R_{X_p}(e)\le2r^{-1/2}.$$ Thus at least $N=\lceil(1-2\delta)M\rceil$ edges satisfy this closed inequality. The condition that at least $N$ edges of the finite set $B$ obey it is closed: it is a finite union, over $N$-element subsets of $B$, of finite intersections of closed conditions. The same count therefore holds at the limit $X$. Since $2r^{-1/2}<s$, these edges belong to $F_s(X)$.

Suppose $\mathcal P_s(X)$ has $d>1$ parts. The original $r$ packed trees in $H$ give $M\ge r(d-1)$. Choose the absolute threshold on $r$ so that $2C_0r^{-1/4}<u$. Then $$|E_{F_s(X)}(\mathcal P_s(X))|
 \ge(1-2C_0r^{-1/4})M
 >q(d-1),$$ contradicting Lemma <a href="#lem:packing-parts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:packing-parts">5</a>(iii). The partition is therefore $\{V\}$, so $F_s(X)$ is $q$-tree-packable. Every one of its edges has resistance at most $s<4r^{-1/2}$, and $X\in\mathcal K$ gives <a href="#eq:shortcut-X" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shortcut-X">[eq:shortcut-X]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-uniform-extraction"></a>
<div id="rem:uniform-extraction" class="remark">

*Remark 10*. For a fixed graph, the finite-cover scale needed to stabilize the chosen threshold can be arbitrarily small. This does not affect the absolute cutoff on $r$. The latter comes only from the hierarchy estimate and the numerical inequalities in the proof. The construction makes no claim about its computational cost.

</div>

<a id="sec:auxiliary"></a>
### A vector representation that certifies tree packings

The next representation gives a matrix inequality sufficient for all the partition inequalities in Theorem <a href="#thm:packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:packing">4</a>. Its construction uses strictly positive edge weights, which preserve every subset rank. The normalization uses the log-determinant variational method behind radial isotropic position (Barthe 1998, sec. 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Barthe1998](#ref-Barthe1998)]. Here the target vector of edge weights is the average of the edge-set indicator vectors of the given disjoint trees. We give the approximate positive-weight form that keeps every subset rank unchanged.

<a id="stmt-lem-auxiliary-representation"></a>
<div id="lem:auxiliary-representation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Isotropic representation preserving ranks). *Let $B$ be the union of $q\geq1$ edge-disjoint spanning trees on an $n$-vertex set, where $n\geq2$. For every $\rho>0$ there are vectors $z_e\in\mathbb R^{n-1}$, indexed by the individual edges of $B$, such that <a id="eq:auxiliary-isotropy"></a>$$\sum_{e\in B}z_ez_e^{\mathsf T}=I_{n-1},
 \qquad \|z_e\|^2\leq\frac{1+\rho}{q}.
 \label{eq:auxiliary-isotropy}$$ Moreover, there are an invertible linear map $M:\mathbf1^\perp\to
\mathbb R^{n-1}$ and positive numbers $s_e$ such that $z_e=s_eMb_e$. Consequently the incidence vectors and the $z_e$ have the same rank on every subset of edges.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-auxiliary-representation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-auxiliary-representation</summary>

*Proof.* Choose orthonormal coordinates on $\mathbf1^\perp$ and write $b_e$ for the incidence columns in these coordinates. Denote the given trees by $T_1,\ldots,T_q$. For $a\in\mathbb R^{E(B)}$, set $$N(a)=\sum_{e\in B}\exp(a_e)b_eb_e^{\mathsf T},
 \qquad
 h(a)=\log\det N(a)-\frac1q\sum_{e\in B}a_e.$$ Every $N(a)$ is positive definite: the columns of $T_1$ are a basis and all its weights are positive. Thus $h$ is a smooth, everywhere finite function.

Let $c_t>0$ be the square of the determinant of the basis of columns indexed by $T_t$. Cauchy–Binet, followed by the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \det N(a)
 &\geq\sum_{t=1}^q c_t\exp\left(\sum_{e\in T_t}a_e\right)\\
 &\geq q\left(\prod_{t=1}^q c_t\right)^{1/q}
       \exp\left(\frac1q\sum_{e\in B}a_e\right).
\end{aligned}$$ Here the last exponent uses the fact that the $T_t$ partition $E(B)$. In particular, $$h(a)\geq L:=\log q+\frac1q\sum_{t=1}^q\log c_t.$$ For $\gamma>0$, the function $h(a)+\gamma\|a\|^2$ is continuous and coercive, hence has a minimizer $a^\gamma\in\mathbb R^{E(B)}$. Comparison with $a=0$ shows that, with $K=h(0)-L\geq0$, $$\gamma\|a^\gamma\|^2\leq K,
 \qquad
 \|\nabla h(a^\gamma)\|
 =2\gamma\|a^\gamma\|\leq2\sqrt{\gamma K}.$$ The stationarity equation supplies the equality in the second formula. Thus one may choose a finite $a=a^\gamma$ for which every coordinate of $\nabla h(a)$ has absolute value at most $\rho/q$. This remains valid when $K=0$, since the displayed gradient bound is then zero.

The determinant derivative formula yields $$\frac{\partial h}{\partial a_e}
 =\exp(a_e)b_e^{\mathsf T}N(a)^{-1}b_e-\frac1q.$$ Define $z_e=\exp(a_e/2)N(a)^{-1/2}b_e$. Their outer products sum exactly to the identity, and the gradient bound gives their asserted norm bound. The common map $N(a)^{-1/2}$ is invertible and every $\exp(a_e/2)$ is positive. These operations preserve all subset ranks, including the dependencies among parallel edges. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-projected-trace-packing"></a>
<div id="lem:projected-trace-packing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Projected trace and packing). *With the vectors in Lemma <a href="#lem:auxiliary-representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:auxiliary-representation">11</a>, suppose $A\subseteq E(B)$ and $c>0$ satisfy $$\sum_{e\in A}z_ez_e^{\mathsf T}\succeq cI_{n-1}.$$ Then $(V,A)$ contains at least $\lfloor cq/(1+\rho)\rfloor$ edge-disjoint spanning trees.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-projected-trace-packing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-projected-trace-packing</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a partition $\mathcal P=\{V_1,\ldots,V_d\}$ into nonempty parts. Let $W$ be the span of the $z_e$ indexed by all edges of $B$ whose endpoints lie in one part. The corresponding incidence vectors lie in the direct sum of the zero-sum spaces on the parts. The representation therefore gives $$\dim W\leq\sum_{j=1}^d(|V_j|-1)=n-d.$$ Let $P$ be the orthogonal projection onto $W^\perp$, so that $\operatorname{tr}P\geq d-1$. Taking the trace after multiplication by $P$ preserves the stated positive-semidefinite inequality: indeed, $\operatorname{tr}(PQ)\geq0$ for positive-semidefinite $P,Q$. Writing $E_A(\mathcal P)$ for the edges of $A$ crossing the partition, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 c(d-1)
 &\leq c\operatorname{tr}P
 \leq\operatorname{tr}\left(P\sum_{e\in A}z_ez_e^{\mathsf T}\right)\\
 &=\sum_{e\in E_A(\mathcal P)}\|Pz_e\|^2
 \leq\frac{1+\rho}{q}|E_A(\mathcal P)|.
\end{aligned}$$ The within-part terms vanish by the choice of $P$. Every partition therefore has at least $\lfloor cq/(1+\rho)\rfloor(d-1)$ crossing edges. Theorem <a href="#thm:packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:packing">4</a> completes the proof; if the floor is zero, the conclusion is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:sparsification"></a>
### One sparsification step

We first derive the exact consequence of the Marcus–Spielman–Srivastava random-vector theorem (Marcus, Spielman, and Srivastava 2015, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[MSS](#ref-MSS)] needed here. The use of deterministic padding permits singular covariance matrices and keeps both halves under simultaneous control.

<a id="stmt-lemma-two-system-split"></a>
<div id="lemma:two-system-split" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:two-sided-split"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Two-sided splitting). *Let $w_e\in\mathbb R^D$ be finitely many vectors, let $\Sigma=\sum_e w_ew_e^{\mathsf T}\preceq2I_D$, and suppose that $\|w_e\|^2\leq\epsilon$ for every $e$, where $\epsilon>0$. There is a subset $A$ of the indices for which $$\frac12\Sigma-\tau I_D
 \preceq\sum_{e\in A}w_ew_e^{\mathsf T}
 \preceq\frac12\Sigma+\tau I_D,
 \qquad \tau=2\sqrt\epsilon+\epsilon.
 \label{eq:two-sided-split}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lemma-two-system-split"><summary>Proof of stmt-lemma-two-system-split</summary>

*Proof.* Independently for each $e$, let $v_e\in\mathbb R^{2D}$ equal $(w_e,0)$ or $(0,w_e)$, each with probability $1/2$. Then $$M:=\sum_e\mathbb E[v_ev_e^{\mathsf T}]
   =\operatorname{diag}(\Sigma/2,\Sigma/2)\preceq I_{2D},
 \qquad \mathbb E\|v_e\|^2=\|w_e\|^2\leq\epsilon.$$ To make the covariance exactly isotropic, diagonalize the positive-semidefinite matrix $I_{2D}-M$. For each positive eigenvalue $\lambda$ with unit eigenvector $f$, put $m_\lambda=\lceil\lambda/\epsilon\rceil$ and introduce $m_\lambda$ deterministic vectors equal to $\sqrt{\lambda/m_\lambda}\,f$. There are finitely many such vectors; each has squared norm at most $\epsilon$, and their outer products sum to $I_{2D}-M$. Deterministic vectors have singleton support and are independent of all other vectors. Consequently Theorem <a href="#thm:mss" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:mss">15</a> applies to the enlarged family.

In its successful outcome, let $A$ be the indices assigned to the first block, and set $S_A=\sum_{e\in A}w_ew_e^{\mathsf T}$. Subtracting the fixed padding matrix from the theorem’s conclusion gives $$\operatorname{diag}(S_A,\Sigma-S_A)
 \preceq M+\bigl((1+\sqrt\epsilon)^2-1\bigr)I_{2D}
 =M+\tau I_{2D}.$$ The first block gives the upper bound in <a href="#eq:two-sided-split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-sided-split">[eq:two-sided-split]</a>; the second block gives its lower bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-step"></a>
<div id="prop:step" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:step-packing-count"></a>
<a id="eq:step-cut-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:step-ratio-bound"></a>
**Proposition 14** (Packing-preserving sparsification). *There is an absolute integer $r_{\mathrm{step}}$ such that the following holds for every integer $r\geq r_{\mathrm{step}}$. If $H$ has at least two vertices and contains $r$ edge-disjoint spanning trees, set $$u=r^{-1/16},\qquad q=\lfloor(1-u)r\rfloor,
 \qquad \epsilon=6r^{-1/2},\qquad
 \tau=2\sqrt\epsilon+\epsilon.$$ Then $H$ has a spanning subgraph $H'$ containing at least $$r'=\left\lfloor\frac{(1-4u)r}{2}\right\rfloor
 \label{eq:step-packing-count}$$ edge-disjoint spanning trees, such that every nonempty proper $S\subset V(H)$ satisfies $$|\delta_{H'}(S)|\leq
 \left(\frac12+3\tau\right)|\delta_H(S)|.
 \label{eq:step-cut-bound}$$ The cutoff may be chosen so that, in addition, $$\frac5{16}\leq\frac{r'}r\leq\frac12,
 \qquad
 \frac{\frac12+3\tau}{r'/r}\leq1+15u.
 \label{eq:step-ratio-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-step"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-step</summary>

*Proof.* Write $n=|V(H)|$. Choose the cutoff large enough for Theorem <a href="#thm:extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:extraction">8</a>, and also require $r\geq16^{16}$, so $u\leq1/16$. The extraction theorem supplies a positive definite matrix $X$ with $$X\succeq L_H,
 \qquad
 \mathbf1_S^{\mathsf T}X\mathbf1_S
 \leq3\mathbf1_S^{\mathsf T}L_H\mathbf1_S
 \quad(\varnothing\ne S\ne V),$$ and $q$ edge-disjoint spanning trees all of whose edges satisfy $b_e^{\mathsf T}X^{-1}b_e\leq4r^{-1/2}$. Let $B$ be precisely the union of these trees. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:auxiliary-representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:auxiliary-representation">11</a> to $B$ with $\rho=u$, and put $$a_e=X^{-1/2}b_e,\qquad w_e=(a_e,z_e),
 \qquad\Sigma=\sum_{e\in B}w_ew_e^{\mathsf T}.$$ The covariance includes cross blocks: $$\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}
 X^{-1/2}L_BX^{-1/2}&\displaystyle\sum_{e\in B}a_ez_e^{\mathsf T}\\
 \displaystyle\sum_{e\in B}z_ea_e^{\mathsf T}&I_{n-1}
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ They need not vanish. Nevertheless, for any vectors $x,y$ in the two coordinate spaces, $$\begin{aligned}
 (x,y)^{\mathsf T}\Sigma(x,y)
 &=\sum_{e\in B}\bigl(\langle x,a_e\rangle+
                          \langle y,z_e\rangle\bigr)^2\\
 &\leq2x^{\mathsf T}X^{-1/2}L_BX^{-1/2}x+2\|y\|^2\\
 &\leq2(\|x\|^2+\|y\|^2),
\end{aligned}$$ because $L_B\preceq L_H\preceq X$. Hence $0\preceq\Sigma\preceq2I$ without any invertibility assumption on $\Sigma$.

<a id="eq:same-subset-two-constraints"></a>
Since $q\geq(1-u)r-1\geq r/2$, and $r\geq4$, $$\frac{1+u}{q}\leq\frac{17}{8r}\leq2r^{-1/2}.$$ Thus every $w_e$ has squared norm at most $4r^{-1/2}+(1+u)/q\leq\epsilon$. Choose the subset $A\subseteq E(B)$ supplied by Lemma <a href="#lemma:two-system-split" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lemma:two-system-split">13</a>. The two principal blocks of the *same* inequality <a href="#eq:two-sided-split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-sided-split">[eq:two-sided-split]</a> give $$\sum_{e\in A}z_ez_e^{\mathsf T}\succeq(1/2-\tau)I_{n-1},
 \qquad
 X^{-1/2}L_AX^{-1/2}
 \preceq\tfrac12X^{-1/2}L_BX^{-1/2}+\tau I_n.
 \label{eq:same-subset-two-constraints}$$ Congruence by $X^{1/2}$ in the second inequality yields $$L_A\preceq\tfrac12L_B+\tau X
       \preceq\tfrac12L_H+\tau X.$$ Testing on $\mathbf1_S$ proves <a href="#eq:step-cut-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:step-cut-bound">[eq:step-cut-bound]</a> for $H'=(V,A)$, simultaneously for all cuts.

For the packing assertion, write the errors in terms of $u$: $$\epsilon=6u^8,\qquad\tau=2\sqrt6\,u^4+6u^8\leq u/2
 \quad(0<u\leq1/16).$$ For example, dividing by $u$ and using $\sqrt6<3$ bounds the left ratio by $6(16^{-3}+16^{-7})<1/2$. In particular $c=1/2-\tau>0$, so the first inequality in <a href="#eq:same-subset-two-constraints" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:same-subset-two-constraints">[eq:same-subset-two-constraints]</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:projected-trace-packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:projected-trace-packing">12</a> give at least $\lfloor(1/2-\tau)q/(1+u)\rfloor$ trees. The rounding margin is explicit: $$\begin{aligned}
 &(1-2\tau)\bigl((1-u)r-1\bigr)
            -(1+u)(1-4u)r\\
 &\quad\geq(1-u)\bigl((1-u)r-1\bigr)
            -(1+u)(1-4u)r\\
 &\quad=r(u+5u^2)-(1-u)\geq0,
\end{aligned}$$ where $ru=r^{15/16}\geq1$. Consequently $$\frac{(1/2-\tau)q}{1+u}\geq\frac{(1-4u)r}{2}.$$ Taking floors proves <a href="#eq:step-packing-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:step-packing-count">[eq:step-packing-count]</a>.

Finally, the floor in that formula and $1/r\leq u$ give $$\frac{r'}r\geq\frac12-2u-\frac1r
              \geq\frac12-3u\geq\frac5{16},
 \qquad\frac{r'}r\leq\frac12.$$ Using $\tau\leq u/2$ once more, we obtain $$\frac{\frac12+3\tau}{r'/r}
 \leq\frac{1+3u}{1-6u}
 =1+\frac{9u}{1-6u}\leq1+15u,$$ which proves <a href="#eq:step-ratio-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:step-ratio-bound">[eq:step-ratio-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:iteration"></a>
### Iteration and the thin spanning tree

We now turn the sparsification step into a uniform bound. The estimate keeps track of the tree-packing parameter rather than the number of rounds. The bounded product of losses uses the summable-error principle in the spectral-halving argument of Harvey and Olver (Harvey and Olver 2014, Appendix F, Claims F.3–F.4) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[HarveyOlver2014](#ref-HarveyOlver2014)].

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-step-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-step</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Choose an absolute integer $r_*$ at least the cutoff in Proposition <a href="#prop:step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:step">14</a>. For every $r\ge r_*$, that proposition supplies a positive integer $r'$ satisfying $$\frac5{16}\le\frac{r'}r\le\frac12,
 \qquad
 \frac{1/2+3\tau}{r'/r}\le1+15r^{-1/16}.$$ These are exactly the estimates in <a href="#eq:step-ratio-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:step-ratio-bound">[eq:step-ratio-bound]</a>.

First let $H_0$ be any graph on at least two vertices with $r_0\ge1$ packed spanning trees. As long as $r_i\ge r_*$, apply Proposition <a href="#prop:step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:step">14</a> to obtain a subgraph $H_{i+1}$ with at least $$r_{i+1}=
 \left\lfloor\frac{(1-4r_i^{-1/16})r_i}{2}\right\rfloor$$ packed spanning trees. Stop at the first $j$ with $r_j<r_*$. This happens after finitely many steps because $r_{i+1}\le r_i/2$. Every $r_i$ is positive.

If $j\ge1$, reading the halving inequality backwards gives $$r_i\ge2^{j-1-i}r_{j-1}
 \ge2^{j-1-i}r_*
 \qquad(0\le i<j).$$ Consequently $$\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}r_i^{-1/16}
 \le\frac{r_*^{-1/16}}{1-2^{-1/16}}.$$ Set <a id="eq:constant-C2"></a>$$\label{eq:constant-C2}
 C_{\mathrm{it}}=\exp\left(
 \frac{15r_*^{-1/16}}{1-2^{-1/16}}
 \right)\ge1.$$ For every cut $S$, multiplication of the step inequalities and <a href="#eq:step-ratio-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:step-ratio-bound">[eq:step-ratio-bound]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 |\delta_{H_j}(S)|
 &\le\frac{r_j}{r_0}
   \prod_{i=0}^{j-1}(1+15r_i^{-1/16})
   |\delta_{H_0}(S)|\\
 &\le C_{\mathrm{it}}\frac{r_j}{r_0}|\delta_{H_0}(S)|.
 \end{aligned}$$ For $j=0$ the same inequality holds with an empty product. All these estimates concern the same sequence of subgraphs and hold for every cut simultaneously.

<a id="eq:packing-thinness"></a>
Since $r_j\ge1$, the final graph $H_j$ is connected. Any spanning tree $T$ of $H_j$ satisfies $$\label{eq:packing-thinness}
 |\delta_T(S)|\le\frac{C_{\mathrm{it}}r_*}{r_0}|\delta_{H_0}(S)|
 \qquad(\varnothing\ne S\subsetneq V).$$ This includes $r_0<r_*$: there are then zero rounds, and any spanning tree works because $C_{\mathrm{it}}r_*/r_0\ge1$.

Finally take $H_0=G$. If $k\ge2$, summing the cut lower bounds over a partition into $d\ge2$ parts shows that $$|E_G(\mathcal P)|\ge\frac{kd}{2}
 \ge\lfloor k/2\rfloor(d-1).$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:packing">4</a> supplies $\lfloor k/2\rfloor$ packed trees. If $k=1$, connectedness supplies one tree. Thus in all cases we may choose $$r_0=\max\{1,\lfloor k/2\rfloor\}\ge k/3.$$ Substitution in <a href="#eq:packing-thinness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:packing-thinness">[eq:packing-thinness]</a> proves the theorem with the universal constant $$C=3C_{\mathrm{it}}r_*.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:mss"></a>
### The isotropic random-vector theorem

This appendix proves the precise form of the Marcus–Spielman–Srivastava theorem used in Section <a href="#sec:sparsification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sparsification">6</a>. The proof proceeds through expected characteristic polynomials, an elementary interlacing argument, and a barrier estimate. In particular, the barrier estimate below is proved directly from partial fractions; no representation theorem for bivariate stable polynomials is required.

<a id="stmt-thm-mss"></a>
<div id="thm:mss" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:mss-hypotheses"></a>
<a id="eq:mss-conclusion"></a>
**Theorem 15** (Marcus–Spielman–Srivastava, (Marcus, Spielman, and Srivastava 2015, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[MSS](#ref-MSS)]). *Let $\epsilon>0$, and let $v_1,\ldots,v_m$ be independent random vectors with finite support in $\mathbb C^d$. If $$\sum_{i=1}^m\mathbb E[v_iv_i^*]=I_d,
 \qquad \mathbb E\|v_i\|^2\leq\epsilon\quad(1\leq i\leq m),
 \label{eq:mss-hypotheses}$$ then $$\mathbb P\left\{
 \left\|\sum_{i=1}^m v_iv_i^*\right\|
       \leq(1+\sqrt\epsilon)^2\right\}>0.
 \label{eq:mss-conclusion}$$ Here $*$ denotes conjugate transpose and the norm is the operator norm. In particular, some outcome satisfies the corresponding positive-semidefinite upper bound. Real vectors are a special case.*

</div>

The case $d=0$ is immediate, so the proof below assumes $d\geq1$. We will use polynomial factorization, elementary compactness, and standard finite-dimensional linear algebra. The polynomial and analytic lemmas needed beyond those facts are proved below.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Polynomial limits and stability

Write $\mathbb H=\{z\in\mathbb C:\operatorname{Im}z>0\}$. A nonzero polynomial $p$ in several variables is *stable* if it does not vanish when all its arguments lie in $\mathbb H$. It is *real stable* if, in addition, its coefficients are real. A nonzero real univariate polynomial is stable exactly when all its roots are real: nonreal roots of a real polynomial occur in conjugate pairs.

<a id="stmt-lem-mss-polynomial-limits"></a>
<div id="lem:mss-polynomial-limits" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Limits of univariate polynomials). *Suppose nonzero univariate polynomials $f_\nu$ have uniformly bounded degrees and converge coefficientwise to a nonzero polynomial $f$. If all $f_\nu$ have no zeros in $\mathbb H$, neither does $f$. If all $f_\nu$ are real-rooted, so is $f$. For monic real-rooted polynomials of a fixed degree, their ordered roots depend continuously on their coefficients.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mss-polynomial-limits"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mss-polynomial-limits</summary>

*Proof.* For the first assertion choose $a\in\mathbb H$ with $f(a)\ne0$. Pass to a subsequence on which the degree is constant, and enumerate its roots $\lambda_{\nu,1},\ldots,\lambda_{\nu,e}$ with multiplicity. By compactness of the complex plane together with the point at infinity, a further subsequence makes every root converge, allowing infinite limits. Every finite limit lies outside $\mathbb H$. In the factorization $$\frac{f_\nu(z)}{f_\nu(a)}
   =\prod_{k=1}^e\frac{z-\lambda_{\nu,k}}{a-\lambda_{\nu,k}},$$ a root tending to infinity contributes a factor tending to $1$, and a root tending to a finite $\lambda_k$ contributes $(z-\lambda_k)/(a-\lambda_k)$. The limiting identity factors $f(z)/f(a)$ with all its roots outside $\mathbb H$. For real-rooted $f_\nu$, the same proof uses the extended real line; all finite limiting roots are then real.

For the last assertion, a bounded set of monic coefficient vectors has bounded roots. Indeed a root of $z^d+\sum_{k<d}a_kz^k$ has absolute value at most $1+\max_k|a_k|$, as is seen by summing a geometric series when the opposite strict inequality holds. Any convergent subsequence of ordered root tuples factors the limiting polynomial. Its limiting tuple must therefore be the unique ordered root tuple of that polynomial. This proves continuity. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-mss-stability"></a>
<div id="lem:mss-stability" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Elementary stability operations). *The following assertions hold.*

1.  *If $A_1,\ldots,A_m$ are Hermitian positive-semidefinite matrices and $C$ is Hermitian, then $$\det\left(xI+C+\sum_{i=1}^m z_iA_i\right)$$ is real stable in $x,z_1,\ldots,z_m$. If $\sum_iA_i$ is positive definite, then $\det(\sum_i z_iA_i)$ is also real stable.*

2.  *If $p$ is real stable, then $(1-\partial_i)p$ is real stable.*

3.  *A nonzero univariate polynomial obtained by setting all but one variable of a real stable polynomial to real constants is real-rooted.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mss-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mss-stability</summary>

*Proof.* For the first assertion, when all variables are in $\mathbb H$, the imaginary part of the matrix is $$(\operatorname{Im}x)I+\sum_i(\operatorname{Im}z_i)A_i\succ0.$$ Such a matrix is nonsingular: a nonzero kernel vector would have a strictly positive imaginary part in its quadratic form, a contradiction. The same argument applies to the second determinant because $\sum_iA_i\succ0$. For real arguments the matrices are Hermitian and their determinants are real; consequently the polynomial coefficients are real.

<a id="eq:mss-log-derivative-upper"></a>
For the second assertion, fix all arguments of $p$ in $\mathbb H$, and regard $p$ as a polynomial in argument $i$ alone. This slice is nonzero, and its roots $\alpha_k$ have nonpositive imaginary parts. If it is nonconstant, factorization gives $$\frac{\partial_i p}{p}
   =\sum_k\frac1{z_i-\alpha_k},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Im}\frac{\partial_i p}{p}<0.
 \label{eq:mss-log-derivative-upper}$$ If the slice is constant, the quotient is zero. In either case it cannot equal $1$, so $(1-\partial_i)p$ is nonzero at this point. The operation preserves real coefficients.

For the last assertion, replace each fixed real constant $a$ by $a+\mathrm i\eta$, $\eta>0$. The resulting univariate polynomials have no zeros in $\mathbb H$ and converge coefficientwise to the stated nonzero real polynomial. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:mss-polynomial-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mss-polynomial-limits">16</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Expected characteristic polynomials

<a id="eq:mss-mixed-polynomial"></a>
For positive-semidefinite Hermitian $d$-by-$d$ matrices $A_1,\ldots,A_m$, define $$\mu[A_1,\ldots,A_m](x)
 =\left.
   \left(\prod_{i=1}^m(1-\partial_{z_i})\right)
   \det\left(xI_d+\sum_{i=1}^m z_iA_i\right)
   \right|_{z_1=\cdots=z_m=0}.
 \label{eq:mss-mixed-polynomial}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-mss-expectation"></a>
<div id="lem:mss-expectation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:mss-expectation-identity"></a>
**Lemma 18** (The expectation identity and real roots). *The polynomial in <a href="#eq:mss-mixed-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mss-mixed-polynomial">[eq:mss-mixed-polynomial]</a> is monic of degree $d$ and real-rooted. If $v_1,\ldots,v_m$ are independent finitely supported random vectors and $A_i=\mathbb E[v_iv_i^*]$, then $$\mathbb E\det\left(xI_d-\sum_{i=1}^m v_iv_i^*\right)
 =\mu[A_1,\ldots,A_m](x).
 \label{eq:mss-expectation-identity}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mss-expectation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mss-expectation</summary>

*Proof.* The determinant in <a href="#eq:mss-mixed-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mss-mixed-polynomial">[eq:mss-mixed-polynomial]</a> is stable by Lemma <a href="#lem:mss-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mss-stability">17</a>; applying all the operators preserves stability. Specializing all $z_i$ to zero leaves a nonzero real-rooted polynomial. To see directly both that it is nonzero and that it is monic of degree $d$, the coefficient of $x^d$ in the determinant is $1$, independent of the $z_i$, and every term involving at least one $z_i$ has smaller $x$-degree. The operations leave that leading coefficient unchanged. The specialization can be performed all at once by substituting $z_i=\mathrm i\eta$ and using Lemma <a href="#lem:mss-polynomial-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mss-polynomial-limits">16</a>.

For the identity, let $M$ be any square matrix, let $v$ be a random vector, and put $A=\mathbb E[vv^*]$. The rank-one determinant identity and the derivative formula for the determinant give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\det(M-vv^*)
 &=\det M-\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{adj}(M)A)\\
 &=\left.(1-\partial_z)\det(M+zA)\right|_{z=0}.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\operatorname{adj}(M)$ is the adjugate. These identities hold also for singular $M$: they are polynomial identities in its entries, or follow directly by multilinearity of the determinant in its columns. Apply this formula conditionally to $v_m$, then $v_{m-1}$, and so forth. Independence keeps each covariance $A_i$ unchanged under conditioning.
